PREPACE 
The following English vel'sion of Profess01· Geiger's "Pali Literatur 
tmd Sprac-he" offers more than the German original, for much new 
material, supplied by Professor Geiger himself, has been incorporated 
into it. 
Professor Geiger also secured the permission of the German 
Publishers. 
I distinctly remember that at the first lecture on Pali I attended 
nt the Munich University I was taught the Law of Moro, the corner-
stone of Pali Phonology. 
My astonishment, therefore, can be well 
imagined when I discovered tlrnt many of tbe Czars of Pali in India 
have never even hea.rd of this law! It is nil the more remarkable, 
because Professol' Geiger'p work is well known in our University and 
is in fact one of the text-books prescribed for M.A. students in lluli. 
On account of the language difficulty, however, our students have not 
hitherto been abJe to make full use of it. 
There was thus a elem· 
case for translating Professor Geiger's work into Engiish. 
The translation was completed on 29th June, 1937. 
But the 
Calcutta University Press, always busy with a thousand things, could 
not pay undivided attention to printing it. 
Hence the inordinate 
delay in bringing out this translation, which at least some students of 
Pali are anxiously waiting for. 
In references Lo Prose texts. iine too has been given throughout; 
thus A.I.23 =Atiguttara-Nikt'lya, vol. I, p. 2, I. a. 
My thanks are due first of all to Professor Geiger, my honoured 
teacher and my teacher's teacher. I am grateful also to Professors 
Suniti Kumar Chutterji, Prubodh Chandra Bagchi, Sailendra Nath 
Mitra and Beni Madhab Barua for the interest they evinced in this 
translo tion. 
31st December, Wl-2. 
B.\'l'AirntsHNA Guosu

PALI LITER.A TORE AND LANGUAGE 
INTRODUCTION 
-I. 
By Pali is understood the language in which is composed the 
'ripiiaka, the sacred scriptm·es of ~eylon and Hinter India, and its 
ancillary literature. 
The word Pali however signifies only "text/' 
''sacred text." 1 
If we use this word to designate the language, it 
1s mer~ly a convenient abbreviation for piilibhiisa. 
Synonymous with 
palibhiisli is tantibhasa. 
Pali is an archaic Prakrit, a Middle-Indian idiom, which is charac-
terised by the same peculiarities which distinguish the Middle-Indian 
from 
the Old-Indian. 2 
Pali, however~ cannot be directly derived 
from Sanskrit; for it shows a number of characteristic features 
which suggest its closer relation to Vedic. 
Thus the ger. in -tvana 
(beside -tva}, the forms tehi, yehi= Ved. tebhis, yebhis (as opposed 
to Skr. tais, yais), eLo. 
This ha~ always to be borne in mind when 
in the following Pali forms are compared with Sanskrit forms. 
The former cannot be derived from the latter but stand beside them 
as later formations. 
II. Pali is not a homogeneous language. 
Numerous double 
forms reveal it to be a mixed dialect. 
Dialectical particularisms are 
found in it in large numbers. 
Yett however, stages of development 
associated with peri0ds following one after another can be clearly 
distinguished in the history of the Pali language. 
Four different 
stages can be distinguished : 
1. 
The ianguage of the Gathas, i.e., the metrical pieces. It is 
of 
0a very heterogeneous character. 
On the one hand, it contains 
many archaic speech-forms which 
are distinguished from 
the 
Old-Indian forms only phonologically ; on the other hand, there are alao 
used in it in large numbers such new formations as are wholly cha-
racteristic of Pali, and they are often crossed by the archaic forms 
1 Cf. the expression iti pi piili, e.g., Tb2Co. 618, where piili=plltho. Further, 
p4li ''Racred text" as distinct from aHhakatha, DpvR. 20. 20; Mhvs. 83. 100; Sdhs. 
JPTS. 1890, p. 58°.

PALI LITERA'l1URE AND LANGUAGE 
which may occur side by side with them, not seldom even in one and 
the same verse. In some cases the exigencies of metre might have 
determined the choice of the forms to be used. 
Particularly in those 
cases where verses out of an older language were translated into a 
later one, the use of archaic forms was liberal, because it afforded 
a closer approximation Lo the original. 
2. 
The language of the canonical prose. It is more homogeneous 
and uniform than the language of the Gathas. 
'l'he archaic forms 
diminish more and more in number and partly disappear altogether . 
• 
The use of new formations is no longer accidental or arbitrary as in 
the oldest period of the language, but is governed by more rigid rules. 
3. 
The later prose of the post-canonical Jiterature, as of the 
Milinda-book, the great commentaries, etc. It is based on the 
canonical prose and reflects its artificial and erudite usage. 
'fhe 
difference between the first and the second period is therefore much 
greater than that between the second and the third. 
The latter is 
further characterised by a still more restricted use of the archaic 
forms. 
4. 
The language of later artificial poetry, which no longer 
possesses a homogeneous character. 
'l1he 
au:hors derived their 
knowledge of the language and borrowed the speerh-forms indiscri-
minately from older and later literature, and their propensity to 
archaism and Sanskritism is more pronounced or less in different 
cases. 
III. '!'here is now on the whole u consensus of opinion tha1, 
Pali bears the clear stamp of a "Kunstsprache," i.e., it is a cotLpro-
mise of various dialect,s. 
This has been most emphatically de-
clared by H. Kern. 1 
Minayeff's 2 opinion stands close to his. 
But already E. Kuhn 3 rightly pointed out that the problem is not 
solved merely by defining Pali as an artificial language-its solution 
is only deferred by it. i'Even an artificial and literary language~ 
which on occasions draws materials from all possible dialects, must 
have had as its foundation a particular dialect." For Pali now arises 
the question, which 1·egion of India was the home of that language 
which was the basis of Pali.

INTRODUCTION 
8 
IV. According to the tradition , current in Ceylon, Pali -is 
Magadhi, Magadhanirutti,Miigadhikabhiisii, that is to say, the language 
of the region in which Buddhism had arisen. 
This is very important, 
for strengthened by this argument the Buddhistic tradition makes 
the further claim that the Pali-Tipitaka is composed in the language 
UFed by Buddha himself 1 and therefore in contrast to all other 
collections it alone represents the original canon. 
For this reason 
Magadhi is also called Mulabhasa 2 as the basic language in which 
the words of Buddha were originally fixed, whereas the other versions 
are regarded as secondary variations. 
V. 
Weighf.y arguments have however been urged against 
the view that Pali is a dialectical form of Magadhi or is based on it. 
Precisely the chief distinguishing features of Magadhi; as we know 
them from the grammarians and from 
the inscriptions and _ the 
dramas, are unknown to Pali. 'rhese features are: 1. the mutation of 
every r into l and every B into A, and 2. the ending -e in N. Sg. Masc. 
and Neut. of a-etems and of consonantal stems inflected like them. 
Pali however retains the r (its change into l is indeed frequent but 
not the rule), and possesses no A at all, but only s, and the nominal 
forms mentioned above end in it with -o, or -afli. 
For this reason 
already Burnouf and Lassen 3 contested the theory that Pali is a 
M agadha-dialect. 
VI. Westergaard,' and after him E. Kulm, 5 consider Pali to be 
the dialect of Ujjayini, because it stands closest to the language of the 
Asoka-inscriptions cf Girnur (Guzerat), and also because th~ dialect of 
Ujjayini is said to have been the mother-tongue of Mahinda who preach-
ed Buddhism in Ceylon. 
R. 0. Franke reached a similar conclusion 
by altogether different means. 8 
In his attempt to locate Pali by 
eliminating all those Indian popular dialects which, on account of their 
peculiar linguistic features, cannot be regarded as the source of Pali, he 
finally reached the conclusion that its original home was "a territory,

which could not have been too narrow, situated about the region from 
the middle to the western Vindbya ranges." Thus it is not improbable 
that Ujjayini was the centre of its region of expansion. Sten Konow1 
too has decided in favour of the Vindhya region as the home of Pali. 
In his opinion there is a closer relationship between Pali and Paisaci, 
and differing from his predecessors in the field, particularly from 
Grierson, 2 he seeks the original home of Paisaci not in North-West 
India but in the region of Ujjayini. 
VII. Oldenberg3 considers Pali to be the language of the Kaliiiga 
country. He considers the legend of Mahinda and bis mission to be 
unhistorical. In bis opinion Buddhism, and with it the Tipitaka, was 
introduced into Ceylon rather in course of an intercourse between the 
island and the neighbouring continent extending over a long period. 
As regards the character of Lhe language, he compares it ab'.)ve all 
with that of the inscription of Khandagiri, which, in his opIDi"D, 
agrees with Pali on essential points. 
Also E.~.Miiller' considers the 
Kaliliga country to be the home of Pali. He bases his conclusion on 
the observation that the oldest settlements in Ceylon could have been 
founded only from the opposite mainland and not by people from 
Bengal or therea_bout. 
VIII. A consensus of opinion regarding the home of the dialect 
on which Pali is based has therefore not been achieved. 5 
Windisch6 
therefore falls back on the old tradition-and I am 'also inclined to do 
the same-according to which Pali 8hould be regarded as a form of 
Magadhi! .the language in which Buddha himself had preached. 
This 
language of Buddha was however surely no purely popular dialect, but 
a language of the higher and cultured classes which had been brought 
into being already in pre-Buddhistic times through the needs of inter-

INTRODUCTION 
5 
.,_- mmunication in India. 1 
Such a lingua franoa naturally contained 
elements of all the dialects, but was surely free from the most obtru-
sive dialectical characteristics. It was surely not altogether 
homo-
geneous. A man from the Magadha country must have spoken it in one 
way, and a man from the districts of Kosala and Avanti in another, 
just as in Germany the high German of a cultured person from 
Wiirttemberg~ Saxony or Hamburg shows in each case peculiar charac-
teristic features. Now, as Buddha, although he was no Magadhan him-
self, displayed his activities mainly in Magadha and the neighbouring 
countries, the Magadhi dialect might have imprinted on his language 
its own characteristic stamp. 
This language could have therefore 
been well called Magadhi even if it avoided the grossest dialectical 
peculiarities of this language. As Windisch has rightly pointed out, 
after the death of the master, a new artificial language must have 
been evolved out of the language of Buddha. Attempts were made to 
retain the teachings of Buddha in authentic form, and to impose this 
form also upon those portions which, although derived from the monas-
tic organisations of the various provinces, were gradually incorporated 
into the canon. In connection with the designation of the canonical 
language as Magadhi, Windisch also refers to the !.r111a, the language 
of the Jaina-suttas. 
It is called 
Ardha-Magadhi, i.e., ''half-
Magadhi.'' Now it is surely significant that the Ardha-Magadhi 
differs from Magadhi proper on similar points as Pali. 2 
For Ardha-
Magadhi too does not change the r into l, and in the noun iuflexfon 
it shows the ending -o instead of Magadhic -e at least in many metrical 
pieces. 
On the other hand, as I believe to have myself observed, 
there are many remarkable analogies precisely between lr111a. and Pali 
in vocabulary and morphology. 
Pali therefore might be regarded as a 
kind of Ardha-Magadhi. 
I am unable to endorse the view, which has 
apparently gained much currency at present, that the Pali canon is 
translated from some other dialect (according to Liiders, from old 
Ardha-Magadhi). 
The peculiarities of its language may be fully ex-
plained on the hypothesis of (a) a gradual development and integra-
tion of various elements from different parts of India, (b) a long 
oral tradition extending over several centuries, and (c) the fact that 
the texts were written down in a different country. 
IX. I consider it wiser not to hastily reject the tradition 
altogether but rather to understand it to mean that Pali was 
J For a graphic description, see Rhys Davids, Buddhist Jqdia, p. 140 ff. 
' fiechel, Gramm. d, l?kr. Spr. 1 p. llS,

<!-- Page 22 -->
P.!LI LITERATURE AND LANGUAGE 
indeed: no pure Magsdhi, but was yet a form of the popular 
speech which was based on Magadbi and which was used 
by 
Buddha himself. It would appear therefore that the Pali canon 
represents an effort 1 to reflect the Buddhavacanarti in its ori-
ginal form. 
This theory would have been refuted if it could be 
proved that the Pali canon must have been translated from 
some other dialect. 
Sylvain Levi2 has tried to 
prove 
this. 
He points out a number of termini such as ekodi, Batfl,ghadiseaa1 
etc., in which a sonant appears in the place of a surd. 
From 
these data he infers the existence of a pre-canonical language in 
which the softening of intervocalic surds was the rule. I do not 
con'5ider Levi's arguments to be convincing. Firstly, because all 
these etymologies given by Levi are uncertain. Secondly, because the 
softening of surds takes place not only in the 'jtermini" but also in 
a, large number of other words. 3 
Moreover, in my opinion, no special 
case should be made out of this phonological phenomenon. For 
they merely represent one of the various dialectical peculiarities which 
are met with in Pali. 
Thus, for instance, we find equally fre-
quent cases of the opposite process (hardening of a sonant) as well as 
various other features which considered together prove the mixed 
character of the Pali language . 
.1 X. If Pali is the form of the Magadhi used by Buddha, then 
the Pali canon would have to be regarded as the most authentic 
form of the Buddhavacana-rri, even though the teachings of the master 
might have been preached and learnt from the very beginning in 
the various provinces of India in the respective local dialects. 
This 
conclusion has been drawn-wrongly, in my opinion-from Cullavagga 
V.83.l=Vin. II.139. 
Here it is related, how two Bhikkhus com-
plained to the master that the members of the order were of various 
origins, and that they distorted the words of Buddha by their own 
dialect (sakaya niruttiya). 
They therefore proposed that the words 
of Buddha should be translated into Sanskrit verses (chandaso). 
Buddha however refused to grant the request and added : anujanami

INTRODUCTION 
7 
bhikkhave sakaya niruttiya buddhavaoanafl' pariyapul}iturr,.. Rhys 
Davids and Oldenberg 1 
translate this passage by "I allow 
you, 2 oh brethren, to learn the words of the Buddhas each in his own 
dialect.'' This interpretation however is not in harmony with that 
of Buddhaghosa, according to whom it has to be translated by ,;I 
ordain the words of Buddha to be learnt in his own language 
(i.e., in Magadhi, the language used by Buddha himself).''~ 
After 
repeated examinations of this passage I have come to the conclusion 
that we have to stick to the explanation given by Buddhaghosa. 
Neither the two monks nor Buddha himself could have thought of 
preaching in different dialects in different cases. Here the qu~ion is 
merely whether the words of Buddha might be translated into 
Sanskrit or not. 
This is however clearly forbidden by the Master,. 
at first negativeiy and then positively by the injunction beginning 
with anujiiniimi. The real meaning of this injunction is, as is also 
best m consonance with Indian spirit, that there can be no other 
form of the . words of Buddha than in which the Master himself 
had preached. Thus even in the life-time of Buddha people were 
concerned about the way in which his teaching might be handed down 
as accurately as possible, both in form and in content. How much 
more must have been the anxiety of the disciples after bis death! 
, The external form was however Magadhi, though according to 
tradition it is Pali.•

I. 'fHE CANONICAL LI'l'ERA'fURE 
1. 
Origin and Authenticity of the Canon 
1. 
The Pali canon is known under the name Tipitaka (Skr. 
Tripitaka), i.e., ''Threefold basket," because it consists of three main 
parts : Vinaya-Pitaka, Sutta-Pitaka and Abhidhamma-Pitaka. It is 
the canon of the Theravada school which itself belongs to the 
Vibhajjavadins. 1 
According to tradition, which on essential points is 
probably quite dependable, 2 its compilation began immediately after 
the death of Buddha about 483 B.C., 3 at the council of Rajagaha. 
It was further 
developed a hundred years later at the council of 
Vesali, the chief cause of which was the cropping up of certain wrong 
views which were threatening to undermine monastic discipline. 
At 
the third council under king Asoka (264 to 227 B.C.)' the canon in all 
its essential parts eeems to have been brought to a formal completion.

Pl.LI LITERATURE AND LANGUAGE 
This council is specially associated with the formation of the Abhi-
dhamma1 for according to tradition, the Thera Tissa Moggaliputta is 
said to have recited at it the Kathavatthuppakarai;ia. 
This book 
contains the refutation of 252 different wrong teachings and fa included 
!llD.Ong the works of Abhidhamma. 1 
The third council was also of 
importance on account of the resolution to send missions to neighbour-
ing countries. The tradition is here supported in an int~resting manner 
by epigraphical discoveries. 2 
Mahinda (Skr. Mahendra), the son of 
king Asoka,3 went to Ceylon as the messenger of the teachings of 
Buddha. He brought to Ceylon the canon in its Theravada form. 
2. The gradual formation of the canon may be imagined to have 
ooine about in the way, that in particular monastic orders the memo-
ries of the speeches and dialogues of the master were kept alive so far 
as they were known at all. 
Hence the introductory words evani me 
sutaf?l 
11so have I heard.'' 
At larger gatherings, as at the councils, 
these particular contributions were examined and given monastic 
sanction in favourable cases. 
In this way the material grew up 
continually and was classified into particular collections. 
Such an 
origin of the canon renders it understandable that already at the 
beginning there was given -the possibility of the formation· ot different 
schools.' On the occasion of the second council, therefore, there arose 
in the ch~rch the schism of the Theravadins and the Mahiisarp.ghikas . 
. The mai;n body of the canon therefore should have been collected in 
the first two centuries after the death of Buddha. Titles such as 
dhammakathikat petakin, auttantika, paff.canekiiyika 5 occurring in 
inscriptions of the 3rd century B.C. prove that already at that time 
the canon must have been divided in the same manner as in later 
_days. 
Of the seven texts which were specially recommended for study 
by king Asoka in Bhabra edict1 four or five may be traced in the

THE CANONiCAL LITERATURE 
11 
Pali canon with tolerable certainty .1 It is also very remarkable that 
the name of Asoka, who is so much praised in all Buddhist orders,. 
has never been mentioned in the canon. 
The assumption is justified 
therefore that in Asoka's time the formation of the canon had been 
practically completed. In the first few centu.ries however the canon 
used to be handed down orally. The Tipitaka along with its commen-
f,ary Atthakatha was fixed in writing, according to a notice in Dpvs. 
and Mhvs., which gives the impression of being quite trustworthy, 
only under king Vattagamani, i.e., a few decades before the beg~nning 
of the Christian era. 2 
3. 
Many peculiarities of the Pali canon may be understood only 
if the way in which it came about is kept in view and it is remembered 
that it was handed down orally for nearly four hundred years. As the 
contributions to the canon came from different places at different 
times "\'arious contradictions could not be avoidedt as has been pointed 
out by R. 0. Franke. 3 
We can thus also understand the schema-
tic character of the canon and the numerous repetitions occurring 
in it. It is in the nature of an oral tradition that events and situations 
of common occurrence should be described in stereotyped form in the 
same words. 4 
Much of what repeatedly occurs in the text was 
without doubt compulsory for the monks to learn. I consider as such .. 
the continually repeated synonymous expressions, the discussions 
in · question and answer which almost look like formularies for the 
examinations which the monks had to pass, 5 the parables and similes, 
and the mnemonic verses. It has to be pointed out however that 
apparently even from the beginning the sacred texts were used for 
P?rposes of preaching. 6 Under such circumstances it was unavoidable

Pl.LI LITERATURE AND LANGUAGE 
that those texts which were familiar to every Bhikkhu should again 
1;1nd again thrust themselves into the sacred tradition. 0£ particular 
importo.noe is the fact that not at all very seldom in one and the 
same collection two or more pieces f91low each other having absolutely 
the 1:,ame contents and wbrding, distinguished merely by the names 
of persons and places. 1 It is clear that in these cases we huve to do 
with differences in tradition dating from the very beginning. 
When 
the collection of individual piece'3 began, the redactors quite naively 
arranged the variants one afLer another without trying to solve the 
problem of their authenticity. And finally, also the influence of purely 
literary activity might not have been quite negligible when the 
coJlections were coi:npiled and written down. It is clear that th~ main 
tendency was to offer the material in as full a form as possible. It ia 
not at all surprising therefore that from collections which were already 
existing whole pieces were taken into new collections whenever there 
seemed to be a sufficient reason to do so. 2 It however remains unknown 
whiob of the various forces wa~ most potent in any particular case, 
and it is not at all unlikely that this question will never be solved 
satisfactorily. 
4. It will be clear from what bas been said above, in what sense we 
can speak of the authenticity of the canon. 
None will claim today 
that all that is contained in it is derived from Buddha himself. But 
without doubt it contains a mass of utterances, speeches and teachings 
of the Master, as they were impressed on the memory of the disciples 
in their more or less accurate form. It is however impossible to read, 
e.g., the Mahaparinibba:r;iasutta,3 without get,ting the impression that 
here we are confronted with the actual reminiscences of the last days 
of the Master. 
Other texts, on the other hand, might be imitations of 
existing types, which were at least impregnated with the spirit of 
Buddha. Others again are purely monastic fiction. 
Such is the case, 
for instance, when in the Vinaya all the. individual regulations about

The Vinaya-Pitaka1 contains the monastic rules of the order 
of Buddhist monks. It consists of the following parts: 
1.

1. 
The Suttavibhanga is based on the Patimokkha. 2 This is very 
probably one of the oldest texLs, containing the formulary for the 
ceremony of confession, as it was performed on Uposatha-days in the 
monastic orders. The SV. is a commentary on this text. 
The 
individual transgressions are divided into categories in the order of 
their seriousness: 
pliriijikii dhammii entailing excommunication from 
the order,. Baflighiidisesii dh., aniyatii dh., then plicittiyii dhammii, 
pafidesaniyii dh.t sekhiyii dh. In SV. a story is always given at first 
stating when the particular transgres5tion was committed for the first 
time. 
Then follows in the words of the Patimokkha the respective 
regulation for the expiation of the transgression, as well as a philological 
explanation of it and a casuistic discussion of individual cases and 
possibilities. 
To the MahavibhaJiga meant for the monks there is 
added at the end of the SV. also a Bhikkhu:r;tivibhaJiga of similar

character.-2. The Khandhakas represent the positive counterpart to 
the SV. They contain the regulations which determine the course of 
life within the monastic order. The Mahavagga consists of the first ten 
Khandhakas. Its introductory chapter gives a history of the time, 
from the Sarpbodhi to the founding of the first Sangha in Benares. 
The Cullavagga is but the continuation of the MV. 
At its end are 
added two supplementary Khandhakas (XI and XII) which contain the 
story of the: __ first two Councils. 
Closely connected with the Khandha-
kas are the kammaviicii, which are formularies for the various acts of 
the Church to be undertaken by the order. 
Seven such formu1aries 
are known, of which the upasampadii-kammaviicii, the formulary for 
the ceremony of the initiation of a member into the order, is still much 
used among the southern Buddhists. 1 · All the kammaviicii known 
to us agree with the corret1ponding regulations of the Khandhakas, 
which are themselves only a colJection oi such formularies with more 
detailed explanation and justification.-3. The Parivara, consisting 
of nineteen sections, is of later origin. In all probabi1ity it was 
originated only in Ceylon. 
Thi~ is certain of the introductory verses. 2 
It is a manual of instruction about the contents of the Vinaya-a 
miitikii (table of contents) without any value of its own.

They are the ''collec-
tions'' of Suttas or Suttautas, i.e., of speechen and dialogues of 
Buddha, or occasionally of his first disciples with their followers. Their 
form is prose sprinkled with ver.5es. 
The individual Suttas vary 
according to the place and time of their origin. 
Instead of nikiiya 
also the term iigama II is· used: Dighagama, etc. This is the expression 
universally used in northern Buddhist literature.

is the chief source of our knowledge of the dhamma. It is therefore

The Digha-Nikaya "long collection" contains the longest

As a collection it was complete already at a very early 
time, the Brahmaja.lasutta of D. is quoted in Sa:rpyutta-Nikaya IV. 
28612 R. 0. Franke3 wanted to prove_ that the D. is a "homogene-
ously conceived literary work " and '' a homogeneous work of some 
literatus." This is certainly wrong.' Such a view militates against 
the fact that also inside the D. there are found contradictions just as 
in the canon taken as a whole. 
The external relations which Franke5 
has proved to exist between 
individual Suttas only explain 
why a particular Sutta came to occupy a particular place in the 
collection. Nothing more can be inferred from them. 
A peculiar 
importance attaches to the 16. Sutta of the D., the Mahaparinibbai;ia-
sutta, • a running description of the events of the last weeks of the 
life of Buddha.-The number of Suttas in D. is 84; it is divided into 
three parts (Vagga) : Silakkhandhavagga (1-18), Mahavagga (14-23), 
Pa.tikavagga (24-84).

11. 
The 5th Nikaya of the Butta-Pitaka is the· Khuddaka-Nikaya 
" the collection of short pieces." It contains te.xts of the most diverse 
, characters, and it is significant that among the Buddhists of Ceylon, 
Burma and Siam there is · no complete agreement as to the pieces 
belonging to it. In Ceylon Khud.daka-Nikaya is considered to 
consist of: 1. the Khuddakapatha 2 , a collect.ion composed of only 
9 short Butta-like pieces. It is clearly a prayer book of daily use. 
'fhree of the Suttas (Nrs. 5, 6, 9) occur also in Suttanipata. Quite 
popular in character is the Tirokuq.q.asutta (Nr. 7) which deals with the 
Petas, the departed souls. It bear~ the character of a magic hymn. 
Particular verses out of it are still recited in Ceylon und Siain 
on the occasion of the cremation of dead bodies, as is reported by 
Seidenstiicker.----2. The Dhammapada 3 i8 a collection of 423 memorial 
verses-a famous anthology of Indian Spruchweisheit. According -to 
contents it is divided into 26 parts (vagga). 
Sometimes several conse-
cutive verses form one close group-a small poem in itself. More 
than half the verses may be found also in other canonical texts. 
The 
compiler of the Dh. however certainly did not depend solely on these 
canonical texts but also made use of the great mass of pithy say· 
ings which formed a vast floating literature in India.-3. The Udana' is 
a collection of solemn sayings of Buddha, mostly in metrical form.

were uttered. Altogether we have 82 stories divided into 8 parts 
(vagga)-4. 'rhe Itivuttaka 1 "Thus-has-been-said·• closely resembles 
.the Udana. It contains the Master's sayings on morality. 
The 
number of the individual pieces, which are composed in a mixture of 
prose and verse, is 112. 
'rhey are called Suttas and they either repeat 
the same thoughts at first in prose and then in verse, or in such a 
mamier that the portions in prose and verse supplement each other. 
Like A. the Iv, is divided into Nipiitas (Eka-. Duka-, Tika-, Cotukka-
Nipiita),_ and the latter are again divided into vaggas. 
12. 5. 'l'he Suttanipiita2 of the Khuddaka-Nikiiya is very archaic 
in character. Its first four parts (vagga) consist of 54 pieces; the r.th, 
called Piiriiyal}.avagga; is a running poem in 18 sub-sections, and it 
contains the questions which the 16 disciples of Biivari ask 
Buddha, as well as their answers. 
Some pieces of the Sn. are like 
.lkhyiina-poems in character. Narrative stanzas sometimes alternate 
in them with dialogue-stanzas, as for mstance in the Niilakasutta (III. 
11), or, as in the Selasutta (III. 7), the speeches are in verse and the 
introductory or connecting portions in prose.-6. Vimiinavatthu3 and 7. 
Petavatthu' belong to the later and the least happy parts of the canon. 
Their later origin-perhaps a short time before the third council-
is proved less by their contents than by their language which is not 
aJways impeccable, the mention of Pingalaka, 11 etc. The Vimiina-
vatthu describes the grandeur of celestial palaces, in which the Devas 
live in reward of some good act performed in lifetime. It consists of 88 
stories in seven parts (vagga). 
The Petavatthu consists of 51 stories 
in four vaggas, and it describes the sorrowful fate of the spirits or 
restless souls (peta)~ which _have to expiate for the sins committed in 
lifetime.

13. Like Vv. and Pv. also 8. the Theragatha and 9. Therigatha 1 
are metrical in form. 
These are collections of strophes which are 
attributed to renowned monk-1 (Thera) and nuns ('l'heri). Often several 
strophes together form short poems. 
There is no doubt that thes~ 
st~ophes contain much that beioogs to the authentic Buddhistic litera-
ture of the earliest times. Many verses however might have been fabr~-
cated by the collectors or the redactors on the basis of fragmentary 
rermmscencei3. 
As for the authors, the ascripticn of verses to a parti-
cular monk or a particular nun might in most cases be quite arbitrary. 
It is ho\vever not impossible that in some cases the ascription of verses 
to these authors is based on a dependable tradition. The Thera- and 
the Therigathas can on no account be regarded as a '' homogeneous 
wvrk of a single intellect," even if due concession is made in this 
respect for the notorious utilisation of existing gathiis.' The number 
of Theragathiis is 1,279, and that of 'fherigathas 522; the former are 
divided into 21 and the latter into 16 Nipatas, called Eka-, Dukanipa~A, 
etc., according as they contain one strophe or more attributed to on~ 
and the same author.-10. The Jatakas 3 are a collection of stroph_e,s 
which from the beginning presuppose accompanying prose 1,1arrati~~lil-· 
Each of the latter -contains the story of the Buddha in one of his earH~r 
existences. Only the verses however were regarded as . canonical; .t~e 
prose narrative was left more or less to the discretion of the . re~iters. 
The strophes are very different in character. 
Sometimes they give the 
morals of the story, sometimes the questions and answers of the 
characters in the story form the subject of t,he verses, and sometimes 
the verses represent both the djalogue and the narrative. In the Jat~r. 
Jii.takas the narrative verses are predominant and they ofben combine 
to form ballads or epic poems. In the face of such diversity I cannot 
understand how ''the mass of the Jataka-Gathas as a whole" may be 
regarded as '' the personal product of a single author." 4 
The Jataka 
verses are doubtless a'' collection." This is suggested also by its qui~e

contain merely detailed elucidations of various topics dealing with 
ethics, psychology or theory of knowledge which are mentioned in the· 
canon. Its form is throughout scholastic. 
The themes are schemati-
cally classified; they are not pr.:>perly defined but rather described 
by multiplymg synonyms and they are brought into all possible com-
binations considered as they are ·from the most different points of 
view. 
The Abhidhacnma is highly venerated particularly in Burma.. 
'rhe first suggestions of the Abhidha.mma are found already in the 
dry and schematic enumerations in the Anguttara-Nikaya of the 
Sutta-Pitaka, the last two Suttantas of the Digha-Nikaya1 snd 
similar pieces. 
16. 
The following seven works belong to the Abhidham-ma : 
1. DhammasaiigaJJ.i2 
"enumeration of psychical phenomena, "-a 
psychological work.-2. Vibhanga3 . "differentiation," a supplement 
and continuation of the preceding.-3. Kathavatthu,4 which hag been 
already referred to in 1. It is perhaps historically the most important 
book in the Abhidhsmma. I consider the tradition about its origin to 
be quite trustworthy.-4. The Puggalapaiifiatti5 "description· of 
individuals" deals with the various pers~nalities and.characters in the 
form of questions and answers.-5. 
The Dhatukatha or the Dhatu-
kathapakaraJJ.a8 "discussion of the elementst' deals with the various 
psychic phenomena and their relation to the categories.--6. The 
Yamaka1 ''book of pairs" is a work on applied logic, and it derives its 
name apparently from the fact that all psychic phenomena are consider-
ed in it in the light of a particular thesis and its opposite· antithesis.-7. 
The PatthanappakaraJJ.a or Mahapatthana.8 is a volumin~us work. It 
deals with causality, but is very difficult to understand. The best 
European expert in the Abhidhamma, Mrs. Rhys Davids,_ says of this

Supplement ; The Paritta 
17. The Paritta or Mahaparitta1 is a collection of canonical 
texts for popular magical use. In Ceylon such Paritta-ceremonies (Sgh. 
pirit) are still in vogue. As Seidenstiicker says, such ceremonies are 
performed on various occasions, such as the building of a new 
house, death, illness, etc. s 
On these occasions the texts collected in 
the Paritta are recited. There are altogether 28 pieces, of which 7 
are token from the Khuddakapntha (cf. 11. 1), which seems to have 
been a predecessor of the Paritta. Also in Burma the Paritta is held 
in high esteem by laity. It is not known when the present Paritta 
was compiled. Magical rites are, however, known in Buddhism from 
the earliest times. It is said of Buddha himself, 4 th!lt he taught 
a "snake charm." to his dis.ciples : it is significant that the purpose 
of the charm is to completely envelop the snakes with one's own 
spirit of benevolence (mettena cittena pharitum), so that it may not 
do any harm to anybody. It is also said in the book of Milinda that 
the parittii were taught by Buddha. 5 
Of the six texts which ore 
then cited as examples, five are found in our Paritta. The comment-
ary of the Dhammapada relates8 of a great exorcism, which was 
performed by .lnanda during t-he life-time of Buddha by means of the 
Ratanasutta of the Suttanipata.

the book represents the orthodox point of view of the Theravadins.

Our Milindapafiha is very probably based on 
a Buddhistic Sanskrit work, composed in North-West India about -
the beginning of the Christian era. a 
'l1he translation into Pali .took ; 
place in Ceylon, already before Buddhaghosa who quotes it repeatedly. 
'Ihe original work was much shorter as the Chinese translations show.' 
'l'he real contents of the work ended with Chapter 2, at the end . of 
which we find in our Pali text the remark: Nagasena.Milindaraja-
pa~ha nitthita. Its Introduction too was much shorter in the original 
than in the Pali version. It was considerably extended in Ceylon by 
various additions after existing models.

there were various versions of it in Palj; also the fragments of a

the Dpvs., it must have been composed between 352 and about 450 
A.D. Regarded from the literary point of view it is certainly a very weak 
performance.

R. 0. Franke was quite· 
right in all this. 1 
But he is quite wrong when, confusing form with, 
contents, he refuses to recognise in Dpvs. any historical value. In 
contents the Chronicle is based on the historical portions of ·the old 
Atthakatha. 
That the informations given in it are not quite . 
imaginary 2 is proved by its frequent agreement with the tradition 
current in continental India. 
The Dpvs. is therefore the vehicle of an 
old historical tradition, and it is the duly of historical criticism to 
examine it minutely and assess its worth. 
II Period 
From the 6th to the 11th Century 
22. The beginning of the 2nd period is characterised by commen-
taries which were compcsed on the Tipitaka and which are based on· 
the Ceylonese Atthakatha. Buddhaghosa is the greatest figure in this · 
commentary literature. He was born in a Brahmin family of Northern 
India· and came to Ceylon during the r~ign of· king Mahariiima 
(468-480). 
He studied the Tipitaka and the Atthakatha 'in ~he: 
Mahavihara of Anuradhapura and afterwards showed a very fruitful 
literary. activity. The details about his life as handed down by tradition 
are probably nothing but legends.3

by an introduction called the Nidiinakathii, which contains the story

· 
· 
. In external form the Jataka-commentary is in my opinion tfie 
work of a priest of Ceylon, 1 be he Buddhaghosa 2 or some othESt 
scholar not far from him in time. But the author has· taken bis 
m·aterial from the old Atthakatha. 3 
The Atthakatha again 1s ba~ed 
on oral tradition. There is no doubt that from the very beginning 
the Gathas were handed down together with the stories-without the 
latter the former often give n J sense at all. 
But the two portions 
have been handed down in different manners. The verse portion was 
fixed and unchangenble ; but the prose portion containing the story 
was left more or less to the discretion of the rhapsodists. 
This' also 
explains the contradictions which are sometimes found between the 
ver~es and the prose, and which was certainly present already in the 
Atthakatha. • In short, the Jatakas are of the type of !.khyan:as, 5 
but it is far from the truth that all the Jatakas are of this type. 
Also stories without any verse were incorporated into the collection. 
Such are, for instance, the Jatakas with the Abhisarpbuddbagathiis,• 
in which the Gathas are not placed in the story itself but are 1athered 
on Buddha. at the end in the samodhana. On the other hand, 
particularly in the Jatakas of the last books, in which the connecting 
prose shrinks almost into nothing in comparison with the verses, we 
see how the epic poetry is being gradually developed out of the older 
Akhyanas. 
The Jatakas were utilised at a very· early time for the 
purpose of didactic sermons. This explains their extraordinary 
popularity from the early days of the Buddhist Church ' to the

Along with Buddhaghosa should be mentioned :-1. Buddha-

namesake of his who lived at a later age.-2. Ananda, of continental

The chronicle-literature of Ceylon owes to the second period 
its most important work, the M ahavaryisa of Mahana.ma. 2 It covers 
the same period of history as the Dipavar:p.sa in almost the same order. 
The dry Chronicle has been here made into an artificial epic poem by 
drawing much new material from the Atthakatha. 
'fhe kings 
Devanar:p.piyatissa (247-207 B.C.) and Duttbagamat;1i (101-77 B.C.) 
are the central figures of the first and the second parts respectively of 
the Mahavarpsa. 3 
According to Indian notions the Mahavar:p.sa is a 
commentary on Dipavar:p.sa. 
For this reason the commentary of 
the former is called Tika. 
I think Fleet 4 
was right when he 
connected the passage Mhvs. 38.59 with the origin of our poem. 
In that case the date of its composition would fall in the beginning 
of the 6th century A.D. 
(under king Dhatusena). 
The original 
Mahavar:p.sa ends abruptly in 37.50 with the words Mahavaryiso 
nitfhito. 
The later continuers have evidently destroyed the final 
portion in order to be able to impose their additions.

exception of a few independent notices, a compilation out of older

As I have tried to

30. The grammarian Kaccayana belongs to the age posterior to 
Buddhaghosa. 
His work Kaccayanavyakara't),a or Kacciiyanagandha 2 
is considered to be the oldest Pali grammar. R. 0. Franke 3 has, 
however, shown that even before Buddhaghosa and Dhammapala 
there must have been a fixed grammatical system, which was different 
from that of Khccayana and was perhaps based on the grammar 
of Bodhisatta. ' 
The chief weakness of Kaccayana's system lies in 
the fact that it ignores the historical relation of Pali with Sanskrit. 
He gives an exposition of Pali wholly out of itself. Moreover it 
does not at all give an exhaustive treatment of the linguistic material. 
Kaccayana has naturally nothing to do with Mahakaccayana; the 
disciple of Buddha. 5 
He is also d;fferent from the Katyayann who 
in the 3rd century B.C. wrote the Varttjkas on Pai;1ini's grammar. He 
is moreover to be distinguished from the author of the Netti and the 
Petaka. Without doubt he is later than Buddhaghosa, for otherwise 
the latter would have followed him in his grammatical terminology 
as the author of the classical Pali grammar. 
A higher limit for the 
date of Kaccayana may be obtained from the fact that he utilised, 
besides the Ka.tantra of Sarvavarman and Pai;1ini along with his 
commentators 1 also the Kasika (7th century). 8

The +ika on the Mahavarpsa ' is of gre"'t importance for 
information about the Ceylonese tradition. It is called Va,p,attha-
ppakasini. s Its author is unknown. 
As for its date, an upper limit 
1s furnished by the reference to Dathopatissa II (about 670 A.D.), 
and a lower limit by the fact that the Paii-Thiipavarpsa (34.2) quotes 
it profusely. If the Mahabodhivarpsakatha quoted in it is identical 
with our Bodhivarp"a (29.2), then we get a stiJI closer upper limit 
for it. 
The Mhvs1-'i. is certainly older than the first supplement to 
the Mahavarpsa by Dharumakitti (38), because it breaks off at the 
same place where the original Maha vaqisa (28) ends. It is probable 
therefore that the Mhvs+i. was composed in the 12th century. lts 
importance lies in the fact that it offers a mass of supplementary 
notices to the Mahavarpsa culled from the Atthakatha. In this way 
it reveals to us the riehness of the contents of the Atthakatha, • which 
was still available at the time. 6

86. 
Vedehathera belongs to the 13th century, as has been proved 
by the researches of d'Alwis and Sten Konow.' 
According to the 
notices in bis works, he belonged to the Brahmin family of Vippagiima. 
He was a II forest-dwelier" and a pupil of Thera Ananda also called 
armlnayatana. 
His works 
are: (a) The Samantakiifavm;iti,ana II de-
sc-ription of the Adam's Peak." 2 
·rhe poem deals with the story of 
Buddha's life and particularly the legends of his three visits to -the 
island of Ceylon. 
On the occasion: of his third visit he left on the 
summit of the Samantakuta his sripada, the print of his left foot. 
The vert;es 722-46 give a descriQtion of the sacred hillt which has 
given the name to the whole poem consist.ing of 796 strophes.-(b) 
The Rasavahini, 3 a collection of proso stories, which, according to 
the introductory words, are based on a Ceylonese original. 
This 
original was translated into Pali by Ratthapii.la in the Mahavihara, 
and the translation was revised by Vedehathora. 
Altogether the 
Rasav. contains 108 stories, of which 40 are derived from the J ambu-
dipa, the continent of India, and 63 from Lankadipu, Ceylon. 
'fhese 
ore fables, ltgends, sagas and religious tales. 
'l'he original work had 
very probably drawn the material from the Atthakatha. Hence the 
frequent quotations with tenahu pora'{l,a. 
In the present Pali version 
the Mahavarpsa has been drawn upon profusely; some of the storiea 
are based on the Apadanas and t-he Jataka-book. 
The Sahassavatthu-
ppakarar,ia " the book of thousand stories " was probably in contents 
connected with the Rasa.v. and it is said toha ve been reintroduced 
i11to Ceylon from Bu:rma.'

87. 
A contemporary of Vedehathera was Buddhappiya, the author 
of the Pajjamadhu,1 a poem which in 104 artistic stanzas glorifies 
the external beauty of Buddha and al~o his wisdom. In the last 
stanza but one, the author gives his own name and says that he is a 
pupil of Ananda, who is doubtless the same person as Vedehathera's 
teaoher.-The Attanagaluviharava1fl,sa 2 was c<;>mposed probably about 
the same time as the Pajjamadhu,-in the second half of the 13th 
century. It is written in a mixture of prose and verse and gives the 
story of the death of Sirisai-pghabodhi (Mhvs. 36.91 ff.) and his wife 
as well as of the founding of the Attanagalu monastery on the spot 
where they died. 
As the anonymous author (v. 3) himself admits, he 
was induced to ccmpose this poem by the priest Anomadassin, who 
is probably the person to whom, according to Mhvs. 
86.37 f., in the 
reign of Parakkamabahu II (1229-1246), was entrusted the At.tanagalu 
monastery, furnished with new buildings by the minister Patiraja. 
88. 
The Mahiivaq1sa of Mahana.ma (28) was continued under the 
special title Culava1f1,sa '' Short Chronicle '' and made mto a narrative 
covering the whole history of Ceylon. 3 
According to tradition the 
first author to continue the work was Thera Dhammakitti, who ac<',0rd-
ing to Mhvs. 84.12 ff. came to Ceylon from Burma in the reign of 
Parakkamabahu II (first half of the 13th century). An e\•ident supple-
ment to the later Mhvs. begins with Chap. 90.104 after the close of 
the reign of Parakkamabahu IV which began in the year 1284. 
The 
second part of the Mhvs. (Chapa. 37-90) which ends here describes above 
all the glorious age of Parakkamabahu I (1158-1186).' In order to 
round off the concluding portion of the Mhvs., let us mention here 
that in the second half of the 18th century, according to Mhvs. 99.78ff., 
the king Kittisiri had the chronicle extended up to his own time. This 
thfrd part is made up of the Chapters 00 (from verse 105) to 100. 
In Chapter 101 are then given notices up to the arrival of the English 
in Ceylon.

event9 furnished by the reference to it in the Moggallanapaficikapadipa

Some idea of the contents of the Saras is 
furnished by its chapter-headings: the Chaps. 1-3, for instance,

antaradhanani, Chaps. 13-15 with silani, kammafthanani, nibbii1J,a, and

chapter contains a lokasartithiti (cosmology).-2. Somewhat later is the 
Baddhammasarr,,gaha of Dh11mmakitti Mahasiimin. 3

the last of the Dhammakittis known to us, who wai active towards

The Sdhs. in 40 chapters gives a history of the Buddhist church 
(without furnishing much new information) from the Councils in India 
to the period mentioned just above, and ends with a hymn in praise 
of the doctrine and a blessing for its study.

it gives a complete history of the Buddhist church in India till the

where Buddhist missions had been sent. 
The history of Buddhism 
in Aparantarattha, i.e., Burma itself, is dealt with very exhaustively 
in Chap. 6. It bas to be mentioned however that in the Burmese 
tradition, no less than 5 out of the 9 countries, to which missions

To each of these three schools belongs a list of 
roots-the three lists being the Dhiitu.manjitsa, the Dhiitupiifha and the 
DhatvatthadipanI. 
Fur dictionary we have the Abhidhanappadipikli. 
Finally there are also some works on prosody, etc. 
The value which 
these works possess as acceE:sories for the study of Pali has been 
rightly judged by Franke. 2 
'l'hey are not based on the direct know-
ledge of Pali 'as a Jiving and spoken language. 
The authors have 
drawn their material from the literature just as we too have to 
do to-day. 
Their method also is not based on any homogeneous 
tradition reaching back to the days when Pali was actually spoken. 
Moreover they slavishly imitate the model works of Sansk1·it grammar 
and lexicography and take over their system mechanically into Pali. 
Grammatical forms and words of Pali which are found in the 
text-books have therefore to be treated with the greatest caution so 
long as they ore not proved actually to. occur in literature. In all 
these cases the possibility is ever there that we have before us merely 
artificial constructions in imitaLion of ·sanskrit.

18. 
Besides Kaccayana, the Thera Moggallann or Moggalluyana.-3 
was the founder of a new grammatical school. 
His works a1·e 1. the 
Moggallayanavyiikaratia, also called Baddalakkharia, with tbe ·vutti 
belonging,to it, and 2. the Moggallayanapaficika, which was the 
author's own commentary on his grammar. 
This commentary is now 
lo~t. 
For the contents of the grammatical work of Moggallana I 
refer to the researches of R. 0. Franke. It is doubtless superior to 
the work of Kaccayana. 
Moggallii.yana's grammar too is of course 
not free from the defects of indigenous Pali grammatical literature, 
but Moggallana deals with the linguistic material more exhaustively 
and with· greater understanding of the essence and character of Pali. 
There are considerable differences in the flrrangement and grouping 
of the ru!es, as well as in the terminology. 
Besides the· older Pali 
grammars, the Ka.tantra and the· work of Pa:i;iini, Moggallana has 
drawn most upon Candragomin. 
As for the age of Moggallana, he 
himself says in the conduding verses of the Vutti that he composed 
the work in the reign of Paraklrnmabhuja.

'fhe year 1154 A.D. 
js given as the date of the Saddaniti. Its author Aggavatl\SB was 
also called Aggapa:r;uJita the third and waa the nephew of the second 
Aggapai;igita, who himself was a pupil of the first Aggapai;i<J_ita. 
Afterwards Aggavarpsa became the teacher of King N arapatisithu 
(1167-1202).-On the whole, as R. 0. Franke has shown, the 
Saddaniti is based on Kaccuyana. 
From the point of view of contents 
it is hardly justifiable to separate the Saddaniti from the school of 
Kaccayana. But Aggavarpsa utilised for his work also the Sanskrit 
grammars such as those of Pa.r;,.ini, etc. The work of Moggalli\na could 
have been hardly known to him; it was composed probably after the 
Saddanit,i. 
The Saddaniti consists of 27 chapters; the first ejghteen 
are called Mahasaddaniti and the remaining nine Cullasaddaniti. It is 
expressly mentioned in the colophon that this work is based on the 
writings of the A.cariya, as well as on the canonical Jiterature, from 
which therefore it has drawn its materia1.

in which the king Parakkamabhuja (Parakkamabahu I) has been spoken 
of in the concluding verses it is clear that the Abbidhanappadipika 
must have been composed shortly after his reign (1153-1186), that is 
to say, towards the end of the 12th century .-The work is divided into 
· 3 parts: synonyms, homonyms and indeclinables. It consists of 1203 
strophes. The model after which this work was composed was the 
Amarakosa. 
Large portions, particularly in the part on synonyms, 
have been taken in toto from the Amarakosa, and not infrequently 
MoggalJana gives as Pali words straight away those forms which he 
himself constructed from Sanskrit according to the phonological Jaws of 
Pali. R. 0. Franke has shown that beside Amara also another Sanskrit 
Kosa might have been used. It does not seem to me to be conclusively 
proved however that another Pali synonyms-dictionary must have exist-
ed before the Abhidhanappadipika. A 'fikii on the Abhidhanappadipika 
(see 46. 6) was composed about the middle of the 1,1th century .-We

dhammakitti, 1 a metrical list of monosyllablic words closely on the 
model of similar works in Sanskrit. The date of the work is the year 
2000 after Buddba=1465 A.D.

A. 
PHONOLOGY 
1. 
Sound-syBtem and Accent 
§ 1. 
:For the "-Titing of Pali there are used in the Orient various 
scripts: in Ceylon the Sinhalese, in Burma the Burmese, in Siam the 
Kamboja script. 1he Bangkok edition of the Tipiiaka is printed in 
Siamese letters. 
§ 2. 
The sound,system of Pali consists of the folJowing: 
1. 
VO\vels: a, ii, i, i, u, fi., e, o, as well as the nasal vowels a'J?l,, 
i'J?l,, Uffl,, 
2. 
Consonants: Gutturals: k, kh, g, gh, n. 
Palatals: e, ch, j, jh, n. 
Cerebrals: f, th, <!,, dh, ti,. 
Dentals: t, th, d, dh, n. 
Labials: p, ph, b, bh, m • 
., 
Liquids: 1·, l, l, fh. 1 
Semi-vowels :y, v. 
Sibilant: B. 
Aspiration: h. 
Here should be noticed: 1. 
The vowels e, o are of middle length, 
m closed syllables they are short and in open syllables they are long.-. 
2. 
The sign of nasaiisation ('1?l-) corresponding to the anusvara and 
anunasika of Sanskrit is called Niggahita by Pali grammarians. In 
Ceylon at present the Niggahita is pronounced as a guttural nasaJ.-3. 
The consonant l stands for intervocalic i!, likewise fh for iJ,h. 
In the 
MSS. Z and l are always confused. 
Some spellings seem to he arbi-
trary. 
Thus kii(a 'black' is mostly written with{, although it is equi-
valent to Sanskrit kala, probably to distinguish it from kala, 'time.' 
Surely 1 should be written everywhere where an etymological connec· 
tion with cerebrals is npparent.-4. 
rrhe h is, where it stands alone, 
n. consonant. In combination with y,_ r, l, v, or with the nasals, it 
appears to have been pronounced in a particular manner. The gram-
marians in this case call it ornsa 'spoken in the breast.' :i 
' There is no special sign for this sound. U is us11ally indicated by the ligature 
(+h. 
I Minayeff, P Gr. p. 2.

P1LI LITERATURE AND LANGUAGE 
·./ § 8. In its sound-system Pali stands in Prakritic stage when 
compared with Old Indian. A]so Prakrit has given up the vowels f, 
/, as well as the diphthongs ai, au, in all the dialects, and the vowel 
r in most of the dialects excepting Apabhrarpsa. Morecver Prakrit 
like Pali possesses the cerebral consonant I and the middle-length 
vowels e, o. 
Most Prakrit dialects have like Pali only the dental 
B ; the cerebral f is found in none of them, and the pa.lat-al s is absent 
in most. 1 
The original diphthongical character of e, o is brought to light 
by the circumstance that io Sa.ndhi a+ i becomes e and a+ u becomes 
o, Cf. macchasBevodoke gatarr,, = macchassa iva udake g 0 
'like the 
course of fish in water' Ja. I.2958 • 
There was even a tendency to 
use theni (instead of ai, au) as vrddhi-vowels. 
Thus are found forms 
originated inside Pali, such as tepifaka, 'devoted to the Tipifaka'. 
DhCo. III. 384 18
1 Mhvs. 5.84 from tipifaka, and opadhika 'relat-
ing to the substratum of existence· Vv. 34.21, 24= S. I. 233 15 ' 21 from 
upadhf.. 
This strengthening into e, o may take place even in those 
cases where originally there was no i, u. 
Thus pothujjanika 'relating 
to common people' Vin. I.1012 from puthujjana, where tho u corres-
ponds to Skr. r (prthagjana). 
Cf. sosanika 'relating to cemetery' 
Pu. 6921, DhCo. I. 69' from suslina=smaalina. In gelaftna 'illness' 
D .II. 9911, J a.Co. II. 8120 from giliina = Skr. gliina, and in sovatthika 
'bringing prosperity' Vv. 
18.7 from suvatthi=Skr. svasti, e and o 
are the strengthened forms of the svarabhakti-vowels i, u originated 
within Pali. 
Cf. a]so veyylivacca 'rendering service' Vin. 
I.28: 0 
from *viylivata= Skr. vyaprta; veyyakan;ta, 'answering' D. I. 5!5 
from viyakaroti = Skr vylikaroti. 
In the same way a has been strengthened into a within Pali ; 
aiikhalya, lla 'friendliness' .M.I. 446111, Ja. IV. 575 from sakhila; 
bhakufika 'with frowning eye-brows' Vin. III. 1816 from bhakufi= 
Skr. bhrakufi (or bhrkuti). 
§ 4. Nothing has been handed down to us about the nature of Pali 
accent. 
It is, however, improbable that the ancient Indian accent 
was still in force. 
Rather, as Jacobi has suggested also for Prakrit, 2 
the Sanskritic accent was the rule in Pali. 3

2. 
The Law of Mora 
§ 5. In Pali, as generaliy in Middle Indian, 1 a syllable can con-
tain only one mora or two ·morns but never more. The syllable is 
thus either (1) open with short vowel (one mora) or (2) open with long 
vowel (2 moras), or (3) closed with short VO'}'el (2 mr ·as). 
Every 
syllable with a nasal vnwel is considered as closed. 
Long nasal 
vowels do not occur. 
Due to this law, where Skr. has long vowel 
before double-consonance (i.e., in closed syllable), Pali has there either 
(a) short vowel before double-consonance2 or (b) long vowel with the 
foJlowing double-consonance simplified. 
Examples of (a): ji1J,1J,a 'old, exhausted'= jir1J,a. 
Likewise ma,ri,a 
'flesh'== ma,risa, and the final in nadi,ri 'the river' (acc.)= nadim. 
The vowels e, ~o are short in such cases: semha 'catarrh'=AZ,,man; 
oftha 'lip' =offha.-Examples of (b) : liikha 'lac'= lakfii; dig ha 'long' 
=dirgha. In case of the vowels e, ot the orthography in the mss. 
varies not infrequently, such as apekkha and apekhii, 'expectation' 
=apekfii; upekkha and upekha 'indifference' =upekfii ; vlmokkha 
and vimokha 'deliverance' D. II.7028 , A. IV. 80611 --:vimokfa. 
§ 6. 
The Law of Mora hai3 led to various changes. 3

it: 1. There can be in Pali long vowel before single consonant where

vaka 'bark' (instead of *va.kka) D. I.1672 , Vin. III. 3421, Ja.Co I. 
8042 =valka; niyiiti 
'goes away'=niryati.'-2. Pali shows short 
vowel before double-cone:onance where originally there was long vowel

also in Pali. The words and forms concerned are taken from those

Pali.

§ 48. 
Consonant-groups may be divided by svarabhakti according 
to § 29. 
They however remain undivided, 1. if they consist of similar 
consonants or of a mute w1th the corresponding aspirate, or 2. if they 
consist of a nasal with a homorganic mute.

Moreover, in so far as no svarabhakti intervenes, the assi-
milation of consonant is characterisE:d by the rule that the consonants 
of lesser power of resistance are assimilated to those of greater resist-
ing power. 
The power of resistance diminitihes in the order: mutes-

Combination of more than two Consonants 
§ 58. 
Under the influence of the general iaws of assjm1lation, 
groups of more than two consonants are reduced to combinations of 
two.-1. Where a nasal preceding a mute stands at the beginning of 
a group, it remains acc3rding to § 48, and the following con!)onants 
are assimilated and simplified: iinanca ' infinity ' is derived from 
iinantya thr,:mgh *iinaftcca (§ 55); r.andha 'hole' Ja. III. 192211 , A. IV. 
2515, DhCo. III. 37610 is derived from randhra through *randdha; 
kankha 'doubt' from kan.kl}a through *kankkha.-~. 
When a heavy 
consonant (mute or sibilant) stands between light consonants (nasal, 
liquid, semi-vowel) at first the first iight consonant is assimilated to 
the heavy one: macca 'man' is derived from martya through *mattya, 
*matya; pa7),hi(kii) ' heel ' D. II. 1719 , JaCo. I. 49110 from parl}?J,i 
through *pal}l}ni, *pal}ni; akamha 'we did' (§159.III) is likewise derived 
from akar.l}ma. 
Svarabhakti is in evidence in vatuma 'path ' D. II. 
85 , S. IV. 5221 through *vatfma, *vatma=vartman, as well as in 
pa~ani(?) 'heel' Abhp. 277 besides paiihi.-3. In the same manner, 
assimilation and simplifir.ation of the first two consonants take place 
at first in those cases where a light consonant stand's at the end of 
the group, and two heavy consonants or one light and one heavy conso-
nant stand at the beginning of the group : oftha ( § 10) through *ufthra,. 
*uthra=Uffra; tikkha 'sharp' Ju, III. 151\ S. I. 1911 (1, Vin. I. 6211 
through *tikkh,:ia, *tikh,:i,a = tik~JJ,a; diifhii itooth' besides dattha Milp.

§ 68. 
EXTERNAL SANDHI of Pali is fundamentally different, from 
that of Skr1 • 
It is aiways arbitrary. 
Neither does it apply equally 
to all the words of a sentence but only to those which are syntacti-
cally closely connected. 
Windisch rightly said that such a i:andhi, 
as opposed to that of Sanskrit, certainly appears to be older and more 
natural. 
The cases in which sandhi can take p!ace arc the following 
combin11tions: 1. 
subject and the verb of the predicate, 2. verb 
and the object, 3. 
substantive and attribute, 4. attribute and attri-
bute, 5. adverb and verb, 6. noun of the predicate and copula, 
7. 
adverb and object, 8. 
vocative and the word preceding it; 
U. 
particles and pronouns may join in sandhi with preceding or 
follo\ving words. 
In general, sandbi in Pali is much mere frequent in 
verses under the stresei of metre than in prose.

When two similar vowels meet: 1. it+ it become a by 
contraction wheu the second word begins with an open syllable: dugga-
tii.harr,, Th2. 122=duygata aha1r1-. 
If the second word begins with a 
closed syllable, one a is simply elided2 : piyo c' assarr,, M. I. 339 ; 
chat' amha=chata amha JaCo. III. 4Hi'. 
.Frequently however also 
in this case contraction takes place, the length of the resulting vowel 
being retained against the general rule § 5. (cf. § 7): gavassa ca= 
gava as.sii ca, Ja. III. 40821 ; nacceti=na acceti Ja. IV. 16522 ; tassa-
kkhibhedarr,,-tassa akkhi. JaCo. III. 431 8 .-2. 'l'he result is similar 
in the case of f + f. r1 + n; yet here elision can take place also when 
the initial syllable is an open one, and that not only-as demanded by 
Jacobi's 1aw3-when the prefinal syllable of the preceding word is long. 
We have thus conLraction in numerous cMes such as gacckatiti=

also when the initial syllable is open, e.g .• cattar' imiini M. I. 661 and 
even paiicas, upadana1.khandhesu M.I, 61 1 •

Confrontation of vowels and consonants (mixed sandhi): 
1. Frequently an original initial consonant-group at the beginning

III. 9518 ; but sometimes also without the stress of metre and in

7728 ; na-ppajahanti l\L I. 1415 ; na-ppamajjasi JaCo. III. 4244 etc.-2.

Note : Tht3 flexioo of Pili is t]noughout determined by the laws of analogy. The 
old historical forms have been more aoJ more replaced by new formations in cour<ie of

I. Noun (Sub3t:mtiva and Adjective) 
1. Generalities 
§ 75. In Pali the nominal stems have undergone multifarious 
changes. 
Due to th~ phonetic law entailing the eiision of final 
consonants, the consonant stems become vowel ones and are inflected 
like the latter. Thus we get sumeclha 'wise' Dh. 208, Vin. I. 535 
(verse) from sumedhas; lipii 'misfortune' (Loe. Pl. iipasu Ju. II. 31713) 
from i.ipad. 
In this way originated sappi 'buUer' (Abl. Sg. sappim-
hi.i D. I. 201 26) from satpis; acci or acci (the latter form in M. III. 
27317) 'brightness' fonn arcis; Wdi 'like this' from tad1·a, etc.; 
similarly -vijju 'lightning' (N. PI. vijjt1 Mhvs. 12.13) from -vidyut; 
maru 'god' from rnand, etc.-Vcry often the transfer to vowel-fiexion 
is effected by adding it t,o tho consonantal stem. 
The a-flexion has 
supplied most of the types of new formations. 1 
Besides sumedha we 
have sumedhasa (N. Sg. sumedhaso Dh. 29; Fem. sumedhasii Mhvs. 
22. 36). Similarly i.ipadi.i Thl, 371, JuCo. III. 1212 beside apii (in JaCo. 
II. 31721 i.ipiisu is explained by iipadiiBu); vijjuta Loe. PL vijj-uti.isu 
D. II. 13!1°) besides -vijju. 
Analogous stems are sarada 'autumn' 
=sarad; barihisa 'sacrificial grass' D. I. 14129 =barhis; 
sarita 
'flowing stream' D. III. 19626 =sa1'it, etc. In this way the conso-
nantal flexion is reduced more and more, and at the side of older conso-
-. 
nantal forms new forms according to the vowel fiexion appear in the 
language and gradually come to be regarded ns the only possible 
correct forms.

rules of Sanskrit.

On :Hexion : 1. The flexions of short-vowel stems and long-
vowel stems coincide with each other in Pali excepting in Norn. 
8g. 
Here the latter mostly retain their length. But shortening 
too is found in this position, as, e.g., in sassu Vv. 29. 7, 8.-2. As a 
rule f at the end of the stem is changed into iy before vowel-endings. 
The flexion is thus like that of monosyllubic 1-stems in Sanskrit. In 
analogy with it n too 1s then changed into uy. 
Yet, howevert 
there are numerous forms of the I-stems in which f is changed 
into 
y ,-particularly, 1netri causa, 
in the Gatha-dialect; but 
analogous forms occur also in canonical prose. 
Cf. ratya Thl.

Two distinct types are in evidence1 : the old 
one in -in and the new one in -i (flexion according to § 82), which is 
derived either from the stem-form in which the in-stems appear in 
compounds, or from the case-form in Instr. Sg. where both declensions 
show the same form. 
Both the types were living in all the periods 
of the language. Cf., e.g., Sg. Gen. jhayino (from jhayin 'thoughtful')

§ 97. 
Present Participles in -nt. 1. 
Their flexion is distinguished 
from that of the adjectives in -nt fir~tly by the fact that the Sg. Norn. 
has retained the ending -a111, = Skr. -an in the Giithn-language and in 
the canonical prose. 
Thus fimirr,, ' living ' Sn. 427, 482, Thl. 44= 
jivan; kubbarr,, 'making' Ja. III. 27812 =kmvan; vihara,rr,, 'sojourn-
ing' Thl. 485=-vihamn; bhatz,arr,, 'speaking' Sn. 429=bhatz,an etc. 
Similarly jana111, 'knowing, M. II. 92:i=janan; passarr,, 'seeing' M. II, 
924 =paAyan. But beside it the ending -nto occurs already in the oldest 
period of the language: kandanto ' weeping, Thl. 406; patthento 
' desiring ' Thl. 264; gavesanto ' seeking ' Thl. 183; apatikujjhanto 
'not getting angry with it' S. I. 16230 (verse) etc., and frequently in the 
canonical prose: kandanto M. II. 820 , appajiinanfo 'not comprehend-
ing, M. I. 722 • In post-canonical prose the form in -nto becomes 
predominant, and th9.t in -arr,, is considered to be archaic. Hence 
nihanarr,, 'killing ' Ja. II. 4071 is explained by nihananto in the Co., 
as also in similar other instances. In the first two periods of the lan-
guage the fl.axion retained the archaic forms: Sg. Instr. icchata. (from 
icchati 'wishes') Thl. 167 =icchata; Gen. vasato (from vasati 'dwells') 
Ja. III. 17'=vasatas; PJ. Gen. vijarwtarr,,(from vijaniiti 'comprehends') 
Thl. 14; vadatarr,, (from vadati ' speaks ') Vv. 53. 1 (Comm.= 
vadantii.narri) ; Sg. Gen. 
passato=paB!Jafas M. I. 74, viharato M. I. 
927 etc. 
Along with them should be counted also the forms Sg. Gen. 
karoto Dh. 116, 'rhl. 98, 99 and Pl, Gen. lwrotani Vv. 84. 21 (but 
kurutarr,, M. I. 51623). 
They belong to the stem karont- abstracted 
out of the Acc. of the new form kmonta-, and their relation 
to the Acc. karontarr,, is as t,hat of vasato, va.<1atarr,, to vasantarr,, 2 • 
We should also note the Pl. Nom. icchato Thl. 820 for icchanto = 
icchantas. 
At the side oi the older forms there are found, already 
from the Gfithfi-lo.ngunge onwards, forms of the a-flexion: Sg. Gen. 
I JPTB. 1909, p. 166.

§ 103. 
Comparison. 
1. 
Several of the old comparatives and 
superlatives in -iyas and -1,1tha have been preserved. 
rrhus seyya(s) 
=1freyas, plipiya(s)=piipiyas (§ 100. 3); bhiyyo, bhiyo 'more' (adv.) 
Dh. 17 f., Thl. 110, 173, S. I. 10817 etc.= bhiiyas. 'rl1e comp. niceyya(s) 
Sn. 855, 918 has been formed clearly on the analogy of seyya(s). 
Moreover we have the superlatives seftha 'the best' =aref!fha; 
papiftha 'the worst'=piipif!tha; kaniftha 'the youngest'=l,anif!tha; 
jettha 'the eldest'=jye[!fha. Settho in Vv. 64. 33 is used in the 
sense of u comparative. 
As in Skr., so also in Pali, these compara-
tives and superiatives may undergo further gradation 1 : seyyatara 
(§ 100. 3); setthata1·a Ja. V. 1487 ; papitfhatara Vin. II. 511 • 
The 
compound papissil..a is difficult to explain. 
According to Childers 
sub voce it is= piipiyas + ilrn. 
A loss contrai:lted from is perhaps to be 
found in papiyyasika of the technical term tassapii.piyyasikii.-2. 
The 
comparative suffix-fora is very productive in Pali. It seems to have 
almost compJetely ousted the superlative suffix-tama. 
An example 
of the superlative is uiaratama 'the highest' VvCo. 32014 ; sattama 
'the best' Sn. 356 is another. 
Regular examples of the com-
parative are piyatara idearer' Ja.Co. III. 279u, siidutara 'sweeter' 
Sn. 181 (used in the superlative sense in S. I. 21419), bahutara 'more' 
Vin. I. 129' etc. 
There are also new formations such as mahantatara 
'greater' M. III. 17013, JaCo. II. 41716, silavantatara 'more virtuous' 
JaCo. II. 321 , va1J,1J,avantatara imore beautiful' D. ,I. 1821 t in which 
the suffix has been added to the stem extended by a. 
In balavatara 
'stronger' M1lp. 23421 it has been added to the shortened stem. Cf.

'the higher' Thl. 518, varatara 'the more excelJent' DhCo. I. 3321 
and the Adv. pathamatararr,, 'earlier' Vin. I. 30', DhCo. I. 1887 ,

IV. Verbal System 
1. 
Generalities 
§ 120. In verbal forms Pali is still farther removed from tbe 
basic Sanskrit than in nominal fiexion. 
The historical forms are on 
the whole well preserved, particularly in the older periods of the 
language. But the actual life of the language lies in the new forma-
tions, which were created in such numbe:rs, eit.her on the basis of 
analogy, or after existing types, that it is scarcely possible to lay down 
rules covering all the individual cases.-It is a prominent feature of 
Pali distfoguishing it off from Skr., that it has lost the dua] .-The 
medium is also disappearing. · Passive forms too have active endings 
alre~dy in the oldest period of the language. 
The Gatha-language still 
shows, it is true, a considerabl~ number of medial forms. But they are 
in part due to the exigencies of metre, and everywhere bear the stamp 
of archaism. 
They become rare already in the canonical prose, and 
rarer stHl in the non-canonical, in which they are confined only to a 
few fixed forms (e. g. Imp. 2. Sg. in -ssu, Pret. 3. S~. in -ittha). 
The 
medial forms appear again in the later artificial pcetry. 
'Ihe Part. 
Pres. Med. in -miina wae productive in every period of the language. 
,-As for the temporal, the perfect has almo::st completely disappeared, 
leaving but slight traces behind. 
Unlike Prakrit, Pali has retained 
the condv;icmal. ,, Imperfect and aorist are no longer sharply distin-
guished between in Pali. 
Both of them have coincided in the preterite 
which is mostly called ''aorist". The various periphrastic formations 
are of great importance: they are originated by the combination of 
participles, gerunds or verbal substantives with the verbs "to be" or 
"to become", or with other verbs of an indefinite meaning. 
They 
represent quite a characteristic feature of the language.-Tbe modes

<!-- Page 175 -->
WORD-FORMATION 
15~ 
are the same as in Sanskrit. 
The Gatha-language has apparently 
still retained isolated forms of the v edic subjunctive.-. · Another impor-
tant feature is the p1·edominance of a-stems in the present system. 
Their analogy has decided the character of many verbs which are 
athematic in Skr. It is no longer possible to set up a complete system 
of paradigms for the different 'conjugation-classes' as they are distin-
guished in Sanskrit. The present-stems of these classes will be discuss-
ed, as is proper, in connection with one chief paradigm, as supplement 
to it, .. ~-Besides the expansion of the sphere of the a-flexion we have 
also to notice that of e-stems.-The preponderance of the present-
stem is of particular importance. · Tenses, as well as verbal nouns 
etc., which in Skr. are based directly on the root, may be derived in 
Pilli from the present-stem. 
This applies to preterite (aori!t), future, 
past part. in -ta, future pass. part. in -tabba, infinitive in (-tuy_e and) 
-turri, and gerund in -tva, -tviina and -ya. 
Moreover new passive and 
causative stems may be derived from present-stems. 
The innovated 
forms in all these cases appear side by side with the historical 
ones-sometimes rare in comparison with the latter, but occasionally 
even surpassing them in the frequency of use. In particular cases 
the process goes even further, when, for instance, an historically 
attested future or passive-stem is made the basis of furliher new 
formations. 1

5285 (Skr. jiryante); miyyare 'they die' Sn. 575 (Skr. mriyante); 
hannare 'they are killed' S. I. 7622 etc. 
These forms are connected 
with the Vedic prESents like sere, isire, and they correspond to the 
forms in -ire in Pkr. 1--The 1. Pl. Med. deserves special consideration. 
Kacc. 8. 1. 2 and 18 (Senart. pp. 423, 429) gives a-1 ending -mhe, which 
is probably derived from -mahe t.hrough syncope. 
The fuller form 
occurs in bhavi.imahe 2 Mhvs. 1. 60. 
Also the ending -masc (and 
-mhase) seems to have been in existence at its side. 
All the forms 
with this ending have not a subjunctive meaning (cf. below § 126), as 
for instance (na) tappiimase (dassanena ta~) 'we are (not) tired of 
(looking at you)' Vv. 17. 4. which is indicative in meaning (cf. Skr. 
trpyate). 
Same perhaps also with abhinandamase 'we are glad' Vv. 
17. 7; saremhase (according to e-flexion) 'we remember' Th2. 383. 
The ending -mase is evidently the medial counterpart of the Act. Vedic 
-masi, and -mhase looks like a cross of -nihe and -mase.

and verbs which originally did not belong to CI. X were transferred 
to it.

uhadeti 'defiles with dung'; mannesi Pret. Sg. 2. 'you thought' pre-

§ 140. 1. 
An isolated form of athem. flexion after CI. II. is to be 
found in hanti1 'strikes' Sn. 118, 125, Dh. 72, 355, S. I. 1543 (verse); 
also Milp. 2141, besides thematic hanati Ja. I. 43218, Milp. 2201, 2. Pl. 
hanatha Jo.Co. I. 263 3 , Opt. Sg. 3. haneyya Sn. 705 etc. Similarly veti

Future. It is used as Irrea!is of the present and the past. Excepting 
in compounds, the augment seems to be obligatory. The tlexion is as 
in Skr., only the 8. Pl. derives its ending -arrisu from the Aorist (§159.

watched' JaCo. I. 47015.-Sg. 2. abhavissa JaCo. II. ll1S, Ill. 306

4. 
Aorist. 
§ 158. The Aorist of Pali is derived from old Imperfects and 
Aorists. 
Apart from the endings, it is characterised by the augment, 
which is however frequently left out. 
Wackernagel3 bas succeeded 
in formulating definite rules according to which the augment is 
retained or di.·opped: 1. 
The augment is retained by monosyllabic 
verbal forms: adani 'I gave', agii 'he went'. 
Also acc-aga, samajjh-
<tgani 
(besides 
adhi-garri 'fh2. 122).-2. The augment is always 
retained, also in the later language, by dissyllabic forms derived from 
the Imperfect, the simple Aorist or the s-Aorist: agamii 'be went', 
adasi 'he gave\ akasi 'be did', avocurri 'they spolfe'. Also ajjh-agam,ii, 
pacc-assosi, 
piiyiisi.-3. In the two oldest periods of the language 
the use of the augment is arbitrary in the dissyllabic forms 
derived from the -i~-Aor. : alabhirri 'I received' beside labhi 'he 
received.' Omission of the augment is the rule in the post-canonical 
prose: khadi 'be ate,' bhindi 'he broke' .-4. The augment is always 
retained by the :trisyl1abic forms (a) of the extended type (§ 165)

For the rest1 forms of three or more syllables began early to drop 
the augment, at first quite at random, but regularly later in the post-
canonical prose. Thus in the Gatha-language we have sWl apuc~hirrisu 
'they asked' beside pucchirr,.su; but the forms which Iaier predominate 
and finally usurp the field are desesirr,. 'f taught,' khadimha 'we 
ate', kathayirr,.su 'they related.'

§ 170. It remains still to discuss a few isolated forms which may 
be 
interpreted in different ways. 
rrbus we have in Tb2Co. 8526 
(verse from Apadiina) the form dakkhisarr,, 'I saw'. 
As 
dakkh-
=d1akEJ- is already itself a stem of Type III (cf. 
§ 104), the 
transfer to the flexional mode of •rype IV is effected by means 
of the ending -isarr,,. 
Probably the para1lel forms in -isarr,, and -irr,, 
of Type IV have led to the new formation· out of adakkhirr,,. 
Simi-
larly (a)sal<khissarr,, 'I was able fo' M. HI. 1792 \ A. I. 13910 ' 28, Pl. 1. 
sakkhimha D. II. 155\ 8. sakkhirr,,su Mhvs. 8. 23 and sakkhisurr,, 
Mhvs. 23. 11. 
Of cour3e it may also be assumed that the future-stem 
of the roots dars and sak provided the basis to these new formations. 
Dakkhati and sakkhati were no longer felt to futures, but as presents 
{cf. § 136. 3), which now formed their Aorist after Type IV. An 
Aorist stem of Type III is however doubtless the basis of the form

adiisimhii 'we gave' Th2. 518 (Comm, 29517 =adamhii), as well as 
of the form ahesumha 'we existed' M.I. 2651-'. I am therefore inclined 
to consider all the forms referred to as 'double constructions' in which 
both the Types III and IV have crossed each other. 
This hypothesis 
seems to be more probable to me than the assumption that these are 
forms of the si~-Aorist of Skr. 
Also pamiidassarri 'I was unexerting' 
M. III. 17929, A. I. 1891 ' 28 is probably nothing but an elaboration of

facilitated by the form sakkhissatri which immediately precedes it. 
5. 
Perfect 
§ 171. With the exception of a iew petrified forms, the Perfe~t 
has been almost completely eliminated from the Pali language. 
Forms like bubodha susoca1 (but cf. also jagama Ja.. 2032) as they are 
found, for instance, in the artificial poetry, are merely learned reminis-
cences. 
To set forth a paradigm for the Perfect, as is done by the 
Grammarians, is therefore unnecessary. 
The last vestiges of the 
perfect are: aha 'he has said' ( =iiha) Sn. 790, Vin. I. 40211 (verse). M. 
I. 1415, JaCo. I. 12!3 and its Plural iihu ( =ahus) Thl. 188. Dh. 345.

for tho first of which there often appears, parlicularly in the Gatha-
Jnnguage, also the rnffix -tviina. 
The sumx -ya uppenrs particularly 
afte:r compounds, but this rule is not r.o strictly f,Jllowed in Pali as 
in Skr.
